People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi, Dr. [Name]. I guess... what made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself just started feeling flat and heavy. Like, I used to love going on photography walks. I have this old Nikon, you know, the kind that actually takes film? My grandfather gave it to me when I was sixteen. He was a photographer for the local paper, actually. Took pictures of everything—parades, mayors, stray dogs. Anyway, I just stopped initiating those walks. Same with mentoring the kids at the community center, and even keeping a clean calendar at work. I'm an executive secretary, so my calendar is usually my lifeline. But lately, it's all just... heavy. I haven't even bought film for that Nikon in months. I think they still sell it at that one shop downtown, though I heard they might be closing because of the rent increases.

Well, let's see... I'd say it's been about a year and three months now. Yeah, right around then. It all started after my sister—well, her health just took a really strange turn, and her behavior got so unpredictable. I had to step in and start coordinating all her appointments and her care, you know? Which, honestly, was a lot like being an executive secretary, but for family, which is always harder. Sleep? Oh, well... it's been weird, actually. Not that I'm sleeping more, no. It's the opposite. There have been nights where I only sleep, maybe, two or three hours. Well, yeah, actually. It's not just the sleep. I have these stretches where I feel... I don't know, just unusually upbeat. Like everything is just clicking perfectly, you know? My nephew actually built his own computer just to play them—bought all the parts online and put it together on the dining room table. Well, the upbeat stretches... I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. My old boss at the law firm, Mrs. Higgins, she had everything in this very muted sage green. She said it was calming. She was a very calm person, actually, except for when the coffee machine broke. Well, serious problems... I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. I've had to double-check tasks that used to just be automatic, you know? Like setting up the conference room projector. 

Speaking of projectors, we finally got one of those wireless ones. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. I tried to get maintenance to replace it for months, but Dave—he's the head of maintenance—he's always so swamped. He's been dealing with the HVAC system on the third floor. Apparently, a family of raccoons got into the ductwork over the winter. Can you imagine? Raccoons! They had to call animal control, and the guy who came out, he had this huge net on a pole. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. 

But, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. I just don't feel as present or reliable when I'm feeling this way, whether it's the upbeat stretches or the low ones. I just don't want to let anyone down, you know? But yeah, work is mostly okay, just having to double-check myself more.

Yeah, I get really irritable over the smallest things sometimes, even when I'm feeling so energetic.\n\nDoctor: When you have these stretches of feeling unusually upbeat, energetic, and irritable, how long do they typically last—do they go on for several days in a row, or do they happen at the same time as the low, heavy feelings you described earlier?\n\nPatient: Well, the upbeat stretches... I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. But they don't really happen at the same time as the low feelings. It's more like... one or the other. When I'm feeling heavy and just want to stare at the wall, I'm definitely not wanting to organize the office. \n\nThough, speaking of organizing the office, we just got a new shipment of those color-coded file folders. The neon ones? I usually prefer the classic manila, it just looks more professional, you know? My old boss at the law firm, Mrs. Higgins, she had everything in this very muted sage green. She said it was calming. She was a very calm person, actually, except for when the coffee machine broke. She drank this very specific dark roast from a local roaster downtown. It caused a whole uproar in the breakroom... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, how long the energetic moods last. Yeah, usually a few days or a week at a time, mostly separate from the down periods.\n\nDoctor: During those upbeat, energetic stretches, did you ever do things that caused serious problems—like risky spending, impulsive decisions, or conflicts at work—or did others notice a big change in your behavior, to the point where it affected your ability to function normally?\n\nPatient: Well, serious problems... I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. I've had to double-check tasks that used to just be automatic, you know? Like setting up the conference room projector. \n\nSpeaking of projectors, we finally got one of those wireless ones. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. I tried to get maintenance to replace it for months, but Dave—he's the head of maintenance—he's always so swamped. He's been dealing with the HVAC system on the third floor. Apparently, a family of raccoons got into the ductwork over the winter. Can you imagine? Raccoons! They had to call animal control, and the guy who came out, he had this huge net on a pole. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. \n\nBut, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. I just don't feel as present or reliable when I'm feeling this way, whether it's the upbeat stretches or the low ones. I just don't want to let anyone down, you know? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the heavy periods... I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. Maybe a few weeks? It all kind of blurs together when I get like that. It definitely makes work harder, though. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. 

Speaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. Why do they do that? You get used to a system and then—bam—they change the interface. My friend Susan, she works in IT over at the hospital, she says it's because the developers have to justify their jobs by constantly pushing out updates. She's been in IT for twenty years. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. It's just hard to keep track.

I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. But they don't really happen at the same time as the low feelings. It's more like... one or the other. She drank this very specific dark roast from a local roaster downtown. I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. I've had to double-check tasks that used to just be automatic, you know? Like setting up the conference room projector. \n\nSpeaking of projectors, we finally got one of those wireless ones. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. \n\nBut, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. \n\nSpeaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. Well, feeling guilty or worthless... I don't really know if I'd use those words. I just feel heavy, you know? And concentrating is definitely tough when I'm like that. It's like my brain is full of cotton. 

Speaking of cotton, I was trying to buy new sheets the other day. The perfume smells give me such a headache. My aunt Linda used to wear this overpowering floral perfume, I think it was called White Diamonds or something like that. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. I tried making it once, but I walked away for two seconds to answer the phone and the marshmallows caught on fire. It was a disaster. I had to open all the windows and it was freezing outside. 

Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the concentration. Yeah, it's hard to focus when I'm feeling that low. But I don't really think about death or anything like that. Just feeling really sluggish and having a hard time keeping my mind on my work.

I'll just wake up way before dawn, and instead of feeling exhausted, I feel completely awake. Like, I feel ready to just sit down and start answering emails or updating appointment lists for work. \n\nSpeaking of emails, I actually got one at 3 AM the other day from that community center I used to mentor at. They're trying to organize a summer bake sale to raise funds for new basketball hoops. The old ones got rusted out after that big storm we had last October. Do you remember that storm? It knocked out power on the east side for three days. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she had a whole freezer full of venison her son hunted, and she was so worried it would spoil. She ended up having a block party barbecue just to cook it all before it went bad. I didn't go, though. Venison is a little too gamey for me, you know? But anyway... yeah, I'll wake up after a couple of hours and just feel totally ready to work.\n\nDoctor: During this same period, have you also noticed times when your mood felt unusually elevated, energetic, or irritable—more than just feeling 'ready to work' after little sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. It's not just the sleep. I have these stretches where I feel... I don't know, just unusually upbeat. Like everything is just clicking perfectly, you know? I feel like I could organize the whole office in a day. And then, out of nowhere, something tiny happens—like someone moving a meeting back by fifteen minutes—and I just get so sharp with people. It even surprises me. I snapped at the mail guy last week because he left the packages on the wrong edge of my desk. \n\nWhich is silly, because he's a nice kid. His name is Tyler. He's going to the community college for graphic design. He showed me some of his sketches once, these really intricate sci-fi landscapes with floating cities and stuff. Really talented. I told him he should look into doing concept art for video games, because my nephew plays those games all the time and the art in them is just incredible now. My nephew actually built his own computer just to play them—bought all the parts online and put it together on the dining room table. Took him a whole weekend, and my sister was so mad because she wanted to use the table for her sewing project. She makes these beautiful quilts... but anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I get really irritable over the smallest things sometimes, even when I'm feeling so energetic.\n\nDoctor: When you have these stretches of feeling unusually upbeat, energetic, and irritable, how long do they typically last—do they go on for several days in a row, or do they happen at the same time as the low, heavy feelings you described earlier?\n\nPatient: Well, the upbeat stretches... I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. But they don't really happen at the same time as the low feelings. It's more like... one or the other. When I'm feeling heavy and just want to stare at the wall, I'm definitely not wanting to organize the office. \n\nThough, speaking of organizing the office, we just got a new shipment of those color-coded file folders. The neon ones? I usually prefer the classic manila, it just looks more professional, you know? But Mr. Henderson insisted on the neon because he read some article in a business magazine about how bright colors stimulate productivity. I don't know if I believe that. My old boss at the law firm, Mrs. Higgins, she had everything in this very muted sage green. She said it was calming. She was a very calm person, actually, except for when the coffee machine broke. She drank this very specific dark roast from a local roaster downtown. They used to give us a corporate discount, but then they changed ownership and the new guy, I think his name was Greg, he completely revamped the pricing structure. It caused a whole uproar in the breakroom... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, how long the energetic moods last. Yeah, usually a few days or a week at a time, mostly separate from the down periods.\n\nDoctor: During those upbeat, energetic stretches, did you ever do things that caused serious problems—like risky spending, impulsive decisions, or conflicts at work—or did others notice a big change in your behavior, to the point where it affected your ability to function normally?\n\nPatient: Well, serious problems... I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. I tried to get maintenance to replace it for months, but Dave—he's the head of maintenance—he's always so swamped. He's been dealing with the HVAC system on the third floor. Apparently, a family of raccoons got into the ductwork over the winter. Can you imagine? Raccoons! They had to call animal control, and the guy who came out, he had this huge net on a pole. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. \n\nBut, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. But yeah, work is mostly okay, just having to double-check myself more.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those 'heavy' low periods, how long does it typically last, and during that time, how much does it interfere with your ability to function—for example, at work or with daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, the heavy periods... She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. It's just hard to keep track.\n\nDoctor: During those heavy, low periods, have you experienced things like feeling excessively guilty or worthless, having trouble concentrating, or having any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Well, feeling guilty or worthless... Well, the shortest time... I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I don't really track the days like that. Maybe a few days? It's hard to say if it's less than four or more than four. 

Speaking of four, I actually have four clocks in my office now. I know that sounds excessive, but they're all set to different time zones because we deal with international vendors. We have one in London, one in Tokyo, one in Sydney, and of course, local time. The Sydney one is always tricky because of daylight saving time. Did you know they do theirs in the opposite seasons? It's so confusing. My cousin Greg moved to Sydney about ten years ago. He works in marine biology, studying coral reefs. He sends me these amazing underwater pictures. He actually got a special waterproof housing for his camera. He said it cost more than the camera itself! It's bright yellow. 

I used to have a bright yellow raincoat when I was a kid. I loved that thing. I'd wear it even when it wasn't raining, just because it made me feel like a firefighter. My dad was a volunteer firefighter for a while, before he hurt his back. He slipped on some ice outside the firehouse one winter. 

But anyway, yeah, the upbeat stretches... I really couldn't tell you the exact number of days. I just know I feel really energized when it happens, but I don't keep a calendar of it or anything.

Same with mentoring the kids at the community center, and even keeping a clean calendar at work. I'm an executive secretary, so my calendar is usually my lifeline. But lately, it's all just... heavy. I haven't even bought film for that Nikon in months. I think they still sell it at that one shop downtown, though I heard they might be closing because of the rent increases.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—when did you first notice yourself losing interest in things like your photography walks and mentoring?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... I'd say it's been about a year and three months now. Yeah, right around then. It all started after my sister—well, her health just took a really strange turn, and her behavior got so unpredictable. I had to step in and start coordinating all her appointments and her care, you know? Which, honestly, was a lot like being an executive secretary, but for family, which is always harder. Speaking of which, my boss at work, Mr. Henderson, he’s been having health issues too. He’s got this terrible sciatica. He was telling me the other day about this special ergonomic chair he ordered from Germany. Supposedly it took six weeks to arrive and cost a fortune, but he still complains about his back every time he stands up. I keep telling him he needs to stretch, maybe try some light yoga, but you know how stubborn people can be when they get used to sitting a certain way... Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the photography walks. Yeah, about a year and three months since I stopped doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—such as trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—since this started?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... it's been weird, actually. Not that I'm sleeping more, no. It's the opposite. There have been nights where I only sleep, maybe, two or three hours. I'll just wake up way before dawn, and instead of feeling exhausted, I feel completely awake. Like, I feel ready to just sit down and start answering emails or updating appointment lists for work. \n\nSpeaking of emails, I actually got one at 3 AM the other day from that community center I used to mentor at. They're trying to organize a summer bake sale to raise funds for new basketball hoops. The old ones got rusted out after that big storm we had last October. Do you remember that storm? It knocked out power on the east side for three days. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she had a whole freezer full of venison her son hunted, and she was so worried it would spoil. She ended up having a block party barbecue just to cook it all before it went bad. I didn't go, though. Venison is a little too gamey for me, you know? But anyway... yeah, I'll wake up after a couple of hours and just feel totally ready to work.\n\nDoctor: During this same period, have you also noticed times when your mood felt unusually elevated, energetic, or irritable—more than just feeling 'ready to work' after little sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. It's not just the sleep. I have these stretches where I feel... I don't know, just unusually upbeat. Like everything is just clicking perfectly, you know? I feel like I could organize the whole office in a day. And then, out of nowhere, something tiny happens—like someone moving a meeting back by fifteen minutes—and I just get so sharp with people. It even surprises me. I snapped at the mail guy last week because he left the packages on the wrong edge of my desk. \n\nWhich is silly, because he's a nice kid. His name is Tyler. He's going to the community college for graphic design. He showed me some of his sketches once, these really intricate sci-fi landscapes with floating cities and stuff. Really talented. I told him he should look into doing concept art for video games, because my nephew plays those games all the time and the art in them is just incredible now. My nephew actually built his own computer just to play them—bought all the parts online and put it together on the dining room table. Took him a whole weekend, and my sister was so mad because she wanted to use the table for her sewing project. She makes these beautiful quilts... but anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I get really irritable over the smallest things sometimes, even when I'm feeling so energetic.\n\nDoctor: When you have these stretches of feeling unusually upbeat, energetic, and irritable, how long do they typically last—do they go on for several days in a row, or do they happen at the same time as the low, heavy feelings you described earlier?\n\nPatient: Well, the upbeat stretches... I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. But they don't really happen at the same time as the low feelings. It's more like... one or the other. When I'm feeling heavy and just want to stare at the wall, I'm definitely not wanting to organize the office. \n\nThough, speaking of organizing the office, we just got a new shipment of those color-coded file folders. The neon ones? I usually prefer the classic manila, it just looks more professional, you know? But Mr. Henderson insisted on the neon because he read some article in a business magazine about how bright colors stimulate productivity. I don't know if I believe that. My old boss at the law firm, Mrs. Higgins, she had everything in this very muted sage green. She said it was calming. She was a very calm person, actually, except for when the coffee machine broke. She drank this very specific dark roast from a local roaster downtown. They used to give us a corporate discount, but then they changed ownership and the new guy, I think his name was Greg, he completely revamped the pricing structure. It caused a whole uproar in the breakroom... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, how long the energetic moods last. Yeah, usually a few days or a week at a time, mostly separate from the down periods.\n\nDoctor: During those upbeat, energetic stretches, did you ever do things that caused serious problems—like risky spending, impulsive decisions, or conflicts at work—or did others notice a big change in your behavior, to the point where it affected your ability to function normally?\n\nPatient: Well, serious problems... I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. I've had to double-check tasks that used to just be automatic, you know? Like setting up the conference room projector. \n\nSpeaking of projectors, we finally got one of those wireless ones. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. I tried to get maintenance to replace it for months, but Dave—he's the head of maintenance—he's always so swamped. He's been dealing with the HVAC system on the third floor. Apparently, a family of raccoons got into the ductwork over the winter. Can you imagine? Raccoons! They had to call animal control, and the guy who came out, he had this huge net on a pole. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. \n\nBut, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. I just don't feel as present or reliable when I'm feeling this way, whether it's the upbeat stretches or the low ones. I just don't want to let anyone down, you know? But yeah, work is mostly okay, just having to double-check myself more.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those 'heavy' low periods, how long does it typically last, and during that time, how much does it interfere with your ability to function—for example, at work or with daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, the heavy periods... I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. Maybe a few weeks? It all kind of blurs together when I get like that. It definitely makes work harder, though. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. \n\nSpeaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. Why do they do that? You get used to a system and then—bam—they change the interface. My friend Susan, she works in IT over at the hospital, she says it's because the developers have to justify their jobs by constantly pushing out updates. She's been in IT for twenty years. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. It's just hard to keep track.\n\nDoctor: During those heavy, low periods, have you experienced things like feeling excessively guilty or worthless, having trouble concentrating, or having any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Well, feeling guilty or worthless... I don't really know if I'd use those words. I just feel heavy, you know? And concentrating is definitely tough when I'm like that. It's like my brain is full of cotton. \n\nSpeaking of cotton, I was trying to buy new sheets the other day. I went to that big department store at the mall—the one with the confusing escalator layout where you have to walk all the way around the perfume counters to go up another floor. The perfume smells give me such a headache. My aunt Linda used to wear this overpowering floral perfume, I think it was called White Diamonds or something like that. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. I tried making it once, but I walked away for two seconds to answer the phone and the marshmallows caught on fire. It was a disaster. I had to open all the windows and it was freezing outside. \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the concentration. Yeah, it's hard to focus when I'm feeling that low. But I don't really think about death or anything like that. Just feeling really sluggish and having a hard time keeping my mind on my work.\n\nDoctor: When you have one of these upbeat, energetic stretches, can you tell me the shortest amount of time it has ever lasted—was it ever less than four days in a row, or does it always go on for four days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, the shortest time... I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I don't really track the days like that. Maybe a few days? It's hard to say if it's less than four or more than four. \n\nSpeaking of four, I actually have four clocks in my office now. I know that sounds excessive, but they're all set to different time zones because we deal with international vendors. We have one in London, one in Tokyo, one in Sydney, and of course, local time. The Sydney one is always tricky because of daylight saving time. Did you know they do theirs in the opposite seasons? It's so confusing. My cousin Greg moved to Sydney about ten years ago. He works in marine biology, studying coral reefs. He sends me these amazing underwater pictures. He actually got a special waterproof housing for his camera. He said it cost more than the camera itself! It's bright yellow. \n\nI used to have a bright yellow raincoat when I was a kid. I loved that thing. I'd wear it even when it wasn't raining, just because it made me feel like a firefighter. My dad was a volunteer firefighter for a while, before he hurt his back. He slipped on some ice outside the firehouse one winter. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the upbeat stretches... I really couldn't tell you the exact number of days. I just know I feel really energized when it happens, but I don't keep a calendar of it or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. Maybe a few weeks? It all kind of blurs together when I get like that. It definitely makes work harder, though. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. \n\nSpeaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. Why do they do that? You get used to a system and then—bam—they change the interface. My friend Susan, she works in IT over at the hospital, she says it's because the developers have to justify their jobs by constantly pushing out updates. She's been in IT for twenty years. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. It's like my brain is full of cotton. \n\nSpeaking of cotton, I was trying to buy new sheets the other day. I went to that big department store at the mall—the one with the confusing escalator layout where you have to walk all the way around the perfume counters to go up another floor. The perfume smells give me such a headache. My aunt Linda used to wear this overpowering floral perfume, I think it was called White Diamonds or something like that. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. I tried making it once, but I walked away for two seconds to answer the phone and the marshmallows caught on fire. It was a disaster. I had to open all the windows and it was freezing outside. \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the concentration. Yeah, it's hard to focus when I'm feeling that low. Well, yeah, they definitely notice. My wife and the people at the office, they tell me I talk so fast during those times that they can't even get a word in edgewise. They say I just bounce all over the place—like, I'll be rattling on about processing the medical forms, and then suddenly I'm jumping to work deadlines, and then I'm pitching all these new mentoring ideas. 

Speaking of mentoring, we actually just hired this new junior admin, Chloe. She’s fresh out of college and I’ve been trying to show her the ropes, but it’s tough because she’s always looking at her phone. Kids these days with the scrolling, you know? I don't get it. When I was taking some college classes back in the day, we didn't have smartphones. We had those heavy textbooks you had to lug across campus in a backpack. I had this one history professor who made us read three different books on the French Revolution. I actually got really into it for a while. Did you know Marie Antoinette probably never actually said "let them eat cake"? It was some kind of myth or taken out of context. I actually baked a whole cake for my final presentation in that class. It was a red velvet cake, but I used way too much liquid food coloring and it stained my hands pink for a week. My wife—she was just my girlfriend back then—she thought it was the funniest thing. 

But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife and coworkers. Yeah, they say I just talk a mile a minute and jump from topic to topic before they can even speak.

They say I just bounce all over the place—like, I'll be rattling on about processing the medical forms, and then suddenly I'm jumping to work deadlines, and then I'm pitching all these new mentoring ideas. \n\nSpeaking of mentoring, we actually just hired this new junior admin, Chloe. She’s fresh out of college and I’ve been trying to show her the ropes, but it’s tough because she’s always looking at her phone. Kids these days with the scrolling, you know? I don't get it. When I was taking some college classes back in the day, we didn't have smartphones. We had those heavy textbooks you had to lug across campus in a backpack. I had this one history professor who made us read three different books on the French Revolution. I actually got really into it for a while. Did you know Marie Antoinette probably never actually said \"let them eat cake\"? It was some kind of myth or taken out of context. I actually baked a whole cake for my final presentation in that class. It was a red velvet cake, but I used way too much liquid food coloring and it stained my hands pink for a week. My wife—she was just my girlfriend back then—she thought it was the funniest thing. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife and coworkers. Yeah, they say I just talk a mile a minute and jump from topic to topic before they can even speak.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those energetic, upbeat periods, have you ever experienced things like hearing or seeing things that others didn't, having unusual beliefs, or needed to be hospitalized because of how you were feeling or behaving?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I haven't ever had to go to the hospital for it, if that's what you mean. But... yeah, during those really high-energy stretches, things get a little strange. Like, I get this really strong feeling that certain messages I'm typing up, or even just scheduling conflicts on the calendar, mean something special. Like there's a hidden pattern I'm supposed to figure out. And when I'm running on barely any sleep for days, I'll sometimes hear these short comments, just a few words, when nobody is there. 

Speaking of calendars, we just switched to a new scheduling software at the office and it is a nightmare. It's supposed to sync automatically with everyone's phones, but it keeps double-booking the main conference room. We had the marketing team and the legal team walk in on each other last Tuesday. Legal was not happy. The head of legal, Mr. Henderson, he's this very serious guy who always wears these incredibly stiff collars. I don't know how he breathes. He actually collects vintage fountain pens. He showed me one once that he said was from the 1920s and had a solid gold nib. I used to try writing with a fountain pen back in college, but I always ended up with ink all over my fingers. Kind of like that red velvet cake incident I told you about. I guess I'm just messy with my hands. My wife bought me this special pumice soap to keep by the sink, the kind mechanics use? It smells like oranges. 

But anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the hospital and the beliefs. Yeah, no hospital, just that feeling about the calendars and messages, and hearing those little comments when I'm not sleeping much.

I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. But yeah, work is mostly okay, just having to double-check myself more.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those 'heavy' low periods, how long does it typically last, and during that time, how much does it interfere with your ability to function—for example, at work or with daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, the heavy periods... I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. Maybe a few weeks? It all kind of blurs together when I get like that. It definitely makes work harder, though. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. \n\nSpeaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. Why do they do that? You get used to a system and then—bam—they change the interface. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. It's just hard to keep track.\n\nDoctor: During those heavy, low periods, have you experienced things like feeling excessively guilty or worthless, having trouble concentrating, or having any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Well, feeling guilty or worthless... I don't really know if I'd use those words. I just feel heavy, you know? And concentrating is definitely tough when I'm like that. It's like my brain is full of cotton. \n\nSpeaking of cotton, I was trying to buy new sheets the other day. I went to that big department store at the mall—the one with the confusing escalator layout where you have to walk all the way around the perfume counters to go up another floor. The perfume smells give me such a headache. My aunt Linda used to wear this overpowering floral perfume, I think it was called White Diamonds or something like that. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. I tried making it once, but I walked away for two seconds to answer the phone and the marshmallows caught on fire. It was a disaster. I had to open all the windows and it was freezing outside. \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the concentration. Yeah, it's hard to focus when I'm feeling that low. But I don't really think about death or anything like that. I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I don't really track the days like that. Maybe a few days? It's hard to say if it's less than four or more than four. \n\nSpeaking of four, I actually have four clocks in my office now. He works in marine biology, studying coral reefs. He sends me these amazing underwater pictures. He actually got a special waterproof housing for his camera. He said it cost more than the camera itself! It's bright yellow. \n\nI used to have a bright yellow raincoat when I was a kid. I loved that thing. My wife—she was just my girlfriend back then—she thought it was the funniest thing. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife and coworkers. Yeah, they say I just talk a mile a minute and jump from topic to topic before they can even speak.\n\nDoctor: During those energetic, upbeat periods, have you ever experienced things like hearing or seeing things that others didn't, having unusual beliefs, or needed to be hospitalized because of how you were feeling or behaving?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't ever had to go to the hospital for it, if that's what you mean. Well, actually, it's not just when I'm feeling low or when I'm super energetic. There have been these... calm weeks, I guess you'd call them. Times when I wasn't all revved up, and I was sleeping totally fine, not staying up all night or anything. But for more than a couple of weeks during those normal times, I still heard my name being called when nobody was there. And I'd still get this really intense feeling that totally ordinary work comments—like someone asking for a file—were actually these coded tests I had to pass. 

Speaking of tests, I had to take this massive typing test when I first applied for this job. They wanted to see how many words per minute I could do. I think I hit 85, which isn't bad. The keyboard they had me use, though, it was one of those really old, clunky mechanical ones. The keys were so loud, it sounded like a machine gun going off every time I typed a sentence. I actually kind of miss that sound. Now everything is so quiet with these flat laptop keyboards. My nephew, he's super into gaming, and he built his own computer from scratch. He bought this special mechanical keyboard that lights up in all different colors. It pulses to the beat of the music he listens to. He listens to a lot of electronic dance music, which just gives me a headache, honestly. I prefer classic rock. Fleetwood Mac, The Eagles. I saw The Eagles in concert back in... oh, it must have been '98? Or maybe '99. We had terrible seats, way up in the nosebleeds, but it was still incredible. 

But yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, the coded messages and hearing my name. Yeah, that happens even when I'm feeling totally normal and sleeping fine, for weeks at a time. It's not just during the high or low periods.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.2, F31.5, F31.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi, Dr. [Name]. I guess... what made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself just started feeling flat and heavy. Like, I used to love going on photography walks. I have this old Nikon, you know, the kind that actually takes film? My grandfather gave it to me when I was sixteen. He was a photographer for the local paper, actually. Took pictures of everything—parades, mayors, stray dogs. Anyway, I just stopped initiating those walks. Same with mentoring the kids at the community center, and even keeping a clean calendar at work. I'm an executive secretary, so my calendar is usually my lifeline. But lately, it's all just... heavy. I haven't even bought film for that Nikon in months. I think they still sell it at that one shop downtown, though I heard they might be closing because of the rent increases.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—when did you first notice yourself losing interest in things like your photography walks and mentoring?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... I'd say it's been about a year and three months now. Yeah, right around then. It all started after my sister—well, her health just took a really strange turn, and her behavior got so unpredictable. I had to step in and start coordinating all her appointments and her care, you know? Which, honestly, was a lot like being an executive secretary, but for family, which is always harder. Speaking of which, my boss at work, Mr. Henderson, he’s been having health issues too. He’s got this terrible sciatica. He was telling me the other day about this special ergonomic chair he ordered from Germany. Supposedly it took six weeks to arrive and cost a fortune, but he still complains about his back every time he stands up. I keep telling him he needs to stretch, maybe try some light yoga, but you know how stubborn people can be when they get used to sitting a certain way... Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the photography walks. Yeah, about a year and three months since I stopped doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—such as trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—since this started?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... it's been weird, actually. Not that I'm sleeping more, no. It's the opposite. There have been nights where I only sleep, maybe, two or three hours. I'll just wake up way before dawn, and instead of feeling exhausted, I feel completely awake. Like, I feel ready to just sit down and start answering emails or updating appointment lists for work. \n\nSpeaking of emails, I actually got one at 3 AM the other day from that community center I used to mentor at. They're trying to organize a summer bake sale to raise funds for new basketball hoops. The old ones got rusted out after that big storm we had last October. Do you remember that storm? It knocked out power on the east side for three days. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she had a whole freezer full of venison her son hunted, and she was so worried it would spoil. She ended up having a block party barbecue just to cook it all before it went bad. I didn't go, though. Venison is a little too gamey for me, you know? But anyway... yeah, I'll wake up after a couple of hours and just feel totally ready to work.\n\nDoctor: During this same period, have you also noticed times when your mood felt unusually elevated, energetic, or irritable—more than just feeling 'ready to work' after little sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. It's not just the sleep. I have these stretches where I feel... I don't know, just unusually upbeat. Like everything is just clicking perfectly, you know? I feel like I could organize the whole office in a day. And then, out of nowhere, something tiny happens—like someone moving a meeting back by fifteen minutes—and I just get so sharp with people. It even surprises me. I snapped at the mail guy last week because he left the packages on the wrong edge of my desk. \n\nWhich is silly, because he's a nice kid. His name is Tyler. He's going to the community college for graphic design. He showed me some of his sketches once, these really intricate sci-fi landscapes with floating cities and stuff. Really talented. I told him he should look into doing concept art for video games, because my nephew plays those games all the time and the art in them is just incredible now. My nephew actually built his own computer just to play them—bought all the parts online and put it together on the dining room table. Took him a whole weekend, and my sister was so mad because she wanted to use the table for her sewing project. She makes these beautiful quilts... but anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I get really irritable over the smallest things sometimes, even when I'm feeling so energetic.\n\nDoctor: When you have these stretches of feeling unusually upbeat, energetic, and irritable, how long do they typically last—do they go on for several days in a row, or do they happen at the same time as the low, heavy feelings you described earlier?\n\nPatient: Well, the upbeat stretches... I guess they last a few days? Maybe a week? It's hard to keep track, honestly, because I'm just so busy when it happens. But they don't really happen at the same time as the low feelings. It's more like... one or the other. When I'm feeling heavy and just want to stare at the wall, I'm definitely not wanting to organize the office. \n\nThough, speaking of organizing the office, we just got a new shipment of those color-coded file folders. The neon ones? I usually prefer the classic manila, it just looks more professional, you know? But Mr. Henderson insisted on the neon because he read some article in a business magazine about how bright colors stimulate productivity. I don't know if I believe that. My old boss at the law firm, Mrs. Higgins, she had everything in this very muted sage green. She said it was calming. She was a very calm person, actually, except for when the coffee machine broke. She drank this very specific dark roast from a local roaster downtown. They used to give us a corporate discount, but then they changed ownership and the new guy, I think his name was Greg, he completely revamped the pricing structure. It caused a whole uproar in the breakroom... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, how long the energetic moods last. Yeah, usually a few days or a week at a time, mostly separate from the down periods.\n\nDoctor: During those upbeat, energetic stretches, did you ever do things that caused serious problems—like risky spending, impulsive decisions, or conflicts at work—or did others notice a big change in your behavior, to the point where it affected your ability to function normally?\n\nPatient: Well, serious problems... I wouldn't say serious problems. My work has stayed intact overall, mostly. Though, I guess I have missed a few calendar details here and there, which isn't like me. I've had to double-check tasks that used to just be automatic, you know? Like setting up the conference room projector. \n\nSpeaking of projectors, we finally got one of those wireless ones. The old one had this VGA cable that was practically held together by electrical tape. I tried to get maintenance to replace it for months, but Dave—he's the head of maintenance—he's always so swamped. He's been dealing with the HVAC system on the third floor. Apparently, a family of raccoons got into the ductwork over the winter. Can you imagine? Raccoons! They had to call animal control, and the guy who came out, he had this huge net on a pole. He said he usually deals with bats. I actually saw a bat once when I was out doing some night photography. I used to go out to the state park a lot to take pictures of the stars. It's so peaceful out there. \n\nBut, honestly, I've pulled back from photography outings lately. And mentoring, too. I just don't feel as present or reliable when I'm feeling this way, whether it's the upbeat stretches or the low ones. I just don't want to let anyone down, you know? But yeah, work is mostly okay, just having to double-check myself more.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those 'heavy' low periods, how long does it typically last, and during that time, how much does it interfere with your ability to function—for example, at work or with daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, the heavy periods... I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. Maybe a few weeks? It all kind of blurs together when I get like that. It definitely makes work harder, though. I just feel like I'm moving through molasses, trying to get through the emails and the scheduling. \n\nSpeaking of scheduling, we use this software now, Outlook, I'm sure you know it, but they just updated it and moved all the buttons around. Why do they do that? You get used to a system and then—bam—they change the interface. My friend Susan, she works in IT over at the hospital, she says it's because the developers have to justify their jobs by constantly pushing out updates. She's been in IT for twenty years. She actually started out fixing those old dot-matrix printers. Remember those? The ones with the perforated edges on the paper that you had to tear off? I used to love doing that. It was so satisfying. We used to make banners with them for office birthdays. Last week was Mr. Henderson's birthday, actually, and we just got him a store-bought card. It's not the same. But anyway, I don't really know how long the low periods last, to be honest. It's just hard to keep track.\n\nDoctor: During those heavy, low periods, have you experienced things like feeling excessively guilty or worthless, having trouble concentrating, or having any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Well, feeling guilty or worthless... I don't really know if I'd use those words. I just feel heavy, you know? And concentrating is definitely tough when I'm like that. It's like my brain is full of cotton. \n\nSpeaking of cotton, I was trying to buy new sheets the other day. I went to that big department store at the mall—the one with the confusing escalator layout where you have to walk all the way around the perfume counters to go up another floor. The perfume smells give me such a headache. My aunt Linda used to wear this overpowering floral perfume, I think it was called White Diamonds or something like that. Every time she came over for Thanksgiving, the house smelled like it for days. She always brought her famous sweet potato casserole, though. She put pecans and marshmallows on top, but she'd toast the marshmallows under the broiler so they were perfectly golden brown. I tried making it once, but I walked away for two seconds to answer the phone and the marshmallows caught on fire. It was a disaster. I had to open all the windows and it was freezing outside. \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the concentration. Yeah, it's hard to focus when I'm feeling that low. But I don't really think about death or anything like that. Just feeling really sluggish and having a hard time keeping my mind on my work.\n\nDoctor: When you have one of these upbeat, energetic stretches, can you tell me the shortest amount of time it has ever lasted—was it ever less than four days in a row, or does it always go on for four days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, the shortest time... I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I don't really track the days like that. Maybe a few days? It's hard to say if it's less than four or more than four. \n\nSpeaking of four, I actually have four clocks in my office now. I know that sounds excessive, but they're all set to different time zones because we deal with international vendors. We have one in London, one in Tokyo, one in Sydney, and of course, local time. The Sydney one is always tricky because of daylight saving time. Did you know they do theirs in the opposite seasons? It's so confusing. My cousin Greg moved to Sydney about ten years ago. He works in marine biology, studying coral reefs. He sends me these amazing underwater pictures. He actually got a special waterproof housing for his camera. He said it cost more than the camera itself! It's bright yellow. \n\nI used to have a bright yellow raincoat when I was a kid. I loved that thing. I'd wear it even when it wasn't raining, just because it made me feel like a firefighter. My dad was a volunteer firefighter for a while, before he hurt his back. He slipped on some ice outside the firehouse one winter. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the upbeat stretches... I really couldn't tell you the exact number of days. I just know I feel really energized when it happens, but I don't keep a calendar of it or anything.\n\nDoctor: During those upbeat, energetic stretches, did coworkers, friends, or family actually comment that you seemed noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, they definitely notice. My wife and the people at the office, they tell me I talk so fast during those times that they can't even get a word in edgewise. They say I just bounce all over the place—like, I'll be rattling on about processing the medical forms, and then suddenly I'm jumping to work deadlines, and then I'm pitching all these new mentoring ideas. \n\nSpeaking of mentoring, we actually just hired this new junior admin, Chloe. She’s fresh out of college and I’ve been trying to show her the ropes, but it’s tough because she’s always looking at her phone. Kids these days with the scrolling, you know? I don't get it. When I was taking some college classes back in the day, we didn't have smartphones. We had those heavy textbooks you had to lug across campus in a backpack. I had this one history professor who made us read three different books on the French Revolution. I actually got really into it for a while. Did you know Marie Antoinette probably never actually said \"let them eat cake\"? It was some kind of myth or taken out of context. I actually baked a whole cake for my final presentation in that class. It was a red velvet cake, but I used way too much liquid food coloring and it stained my hands pink for a week. My wife—she was just my girlfriend back then—she thought it was the funniest thing. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife and coworkers. Yeah, they say I just talk a mile a minute and jump from topic to topic before they can even speak.\n\nDoctor: During those energetic, upbeat periods, have you ever experienced things like hearing or seeing things that others didn't, having unusual beliefs, or needed to be hospitalized because of how you were feeling or behaving?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't ever had to go to the hospital for it, if that's what you mean. But... yeah, during those really high-energy stretches, things get a little strange. Like, I get this really strong feeling that certain messages I'm typing up, or even just scheduling conflicts on the calendar, mean something special. Like there's a hidden pattern I'm supposed to figure out. And when I'm running on barely any sleep for days, I'll sometimes hear these short comments, just a few words, when nobody is there. \n\nSpeaking of calendars, we just switched to a new scheduling software at the office and it is a nightmare. It's supposed to sync automatically with everyone's phones, but it keeps double-booking the main conference room. We had the marketing team and the legal team walk in on each other last Tuesday. Legal was not happy. The head of legal, Mr. Henderson, he's this very serious guy who always wears these incredibly stiff collars. I don't know how he breathes. He actually collects vintage fountain pens. He showed me one once that he said was from the 1920s and had a solid gold nib. I used to try writing with a fountain pen back in college, but I always ended up with ink all over my fingers. Kind of like that red velvet cake incident I told you about. I guess I'm just messy with my hands. My wife bought me this special pumice soap to keep by the sink, the kind mechanics use? It smells like oranges. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, the hospital and the beliefs. Yeah, no hospital, just that feeling about the calendars and messages, and hearing those little comments when I'm not sleeping much.\n\nDoctor: During your low, heavy periods, do you ever experience similar things—like feeling that certain messages have a special hidden meaning, or hearing brief comments when no one is around—or do those experiences only happen during the energetic, upbeat stretches?\n\nPatient: Well, actually, it's not just when I'm feeling low or when I'm super energetic. There have been these... calm weeks, I guess you'd call them. Times when I wasn't all revved up, and I was sleeping totally fine, not staying up all night or anything. But for more than a couple of weeks during those normal times, I still heard my name being called when nobody was there. And I'd still get this really intense feeling that totally ordinary work comments—like someone asking for a file—were actually these coded tests I had to pass. \n\nSpeaking of tests, I had to take this massive typing test when I first applied for this job. They wanted to see how many words per minute I could do. I think I hit 85, which isn't bad. The keyboard they had me use, though, it was one of those really old, clunky mechanical ones. The keys were so loud, it sounded like a machine gun going off every time I typed a sentence. I actually kind of miss that sound. Now everything is so quiet with these flat laptop keyboards. My nephew, he's super into gaming, and he built his own computer from scratch. He bought this special mechanical keyboard that lights up in all different colors. It pulses to the beat of the music he listens to. He listens to a lot of electronic dance music, which just gives me a headache, honestly. I prefer classic rock. Fleetwood Mac, The Eagles. I saw The Eagles in concert back in... oh, it must have been '98? Or maybe '99. We had terrible seats, way up in the nosebleeds, but it was still incredible. \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, the coded messages and hearing my name. Yeah, that happens even when I'm feeling totally normal and sleeping fine, for weeks at a time. It's not just during the high or low periods.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F25.0"], "note": "Patient reports psychotic-like experiences (hearing name called, delusion-like beliefs about coded messages) persisting for weeks even during euthymic periods, independent of manic or depressive episodes. This rules out F31.2/F31.5 (bipolar with mood-congruent psychotic features only during episodes) and supports schizoaffective disorder, bipolar type, where psychotic symptoms occur both during and outside mood episodes.", "is_final": false}

Well, let's see... the mood stuff, like the really heavy lows and those super energized periods, that definitely started first. The other stuff, you know, hearing things and getting so worried about the signs in the calendars and meetings, that's been going on for roughly ten months now.
